There are so many good, kind people in all of these groups — but unfortunately, not everyone realizes that over time, outside forces have used their identity for political agendas, sometimes even making them the scapegoats in global conflicts. > Jesus said "I came in my Fathers name and was rejected...", but the Antichrist will "come in his own name" and be embraced by Christians and Jews. In other words- The AC's own name is his 'brand'.
  
> 
  
> Many Christians will flock to him as a 'savior' and he will fool even the leadership of the Christian churches. These Christians will raise him up and set him on high.
  
> 
  
> He will fight with the 'King of the South' - North- and attack a nation 'to the East'. He will come to power claiming peace and safety- then go to war.
  
> 
  
> He will make a peace Accord for Israel and her neighbors and then expand this to a 7 year agreement, adding more. Eventually he will stab Israel in the back.
  
> 
  
> The world will look upon him (and follow his every move) in 'wonder'. The word translation in Strong's Concordance from the Greek is more like 'flabbergasted' or shocked or amazed. NOT 'loved'. Christians and some others who are under a "great delusion" will follow and worship him- but not the entire world. 2 Thessalonians 2, Matt. 24:24.
  
> 
  
> He is a 'Vile' person. Daniel 11:21
  
> 
  
> He is boastful: He continually boasts about himself- speaking "great things and then even greater."
  
> 
  
> He will roar like a lion, telling the world that he is the best and most powerful of men. Daniel 7:4
  
> 
  
> He is a liar who will prosper because of his lies:
  
> 
  
> "By his cunning he shall make deceit prosper under his hand, and in his own mind he shall become great." - Daniel 8:25a
  
> 
  
> "...and it will throw truth to the ground, and it will act and prosper." - Daniel 8:12
  
> 
  
> He is a contemptible person:
  
> 
  
> "In his place shall arise a contemptible person to whom royal majesty has not been given. He shall come in without warning and obtain the kingdom by flatteries." - Daniel 11:21
  
> 
  
> He is described as 'stout', heavy-set: "...and a mouth that spake very great things, whose look was more stout than his fellows." Dan 7:21
  
> 
  
> This Antichrist will rise to power "from the Sea". Mar means 'Sea', in Spanish. And in the end he will go into the "Lake of Fire". Lago means 'Lake' in Spanish.
  
> -----------
  
> So yes, he/it is protected by "a God" - the wrong one.

I have to strongly disagree when it comes to Donald Trump. His motivations are not rooted in service, justice, or faith—they are driven by fame, power, and wealth. His so-called fearlessness isn’t courage; it’s entitlement—shaped by a lifetime where privilege and money insulated him from consequences.

The sick and vulnerable he’s tried to strip healthcare from?

The veterans he’s mocked, insulted, and whose benefits he’s attempted to cut?

The allies and supporters he turns on without warning?


His track record speaks volumes—decades of documented infidelity, assault allegations, mocking the disabled, degrading women, demonizing immigrants, and disrespecting virtually every marginalized group. There’s no pattern of charity, no evidence of fighting for the poor or powerless, and certainly no humility or repentance.

He doesn’t uphold the Constitution; he undermines it. He doesn’t revere America’s systems of governance; he seeks to bend them to his will. So ask yourself: who is he really fighting for, besides himself?

I posted this in your previous video, but I wanted to know your thoughts:

This isn't the beginning of WW3. "WW3" and "Nuclear War" are just buzz words and fear mongering. The stakes around the world are high, but they have been this way for a long time. Neither China, Russia, or the US want a nuclear war. The only time we hear about it is when some reporter runs up to a leader and asks them if they're going to drop the bombs, and it's all just for drama in the news to make a buck. 



Realistically, Iran is a pawn, and they've about served their usefulness to Russia and China. Us attacking them has been a plot for a long time to divide our forces, and weaken our presence in areas of interest to Russia and China. That being said, everyone must always be prepared, and that is exactly why Russia and China are building a nuclear contingency bunker in Kazakhstan just in case the war actually does happen for some reason. Secondly, China is probably about to go through a regime change because they've already separated military power from state power due to Xi's declining health, and his military's lack of confidence in his ability to lead. Expect his "successor", Yang Wang, to be pushed away from his military control, which is currently headed by Zhang Youxia. He is a survivalist type General who favors nuclear capabilities that operate even when they lose contact with military command. He also is notoriously decptive on the battlefield, showing you what he wants you to see as a tactic while hiding his true intentions. China will likely use the current shift in military theater to focus more on India, and delay any plans for Taiwan until further changes. 



Worst case scenario is that Israel uses nukes, free of backing from the US or European leadership, and result in a joint collaboration in the dismantlement of their nuclear capabilities. 



Something to also keep an eye on is America's greed for oil in the middle east. Current stakes suggest and reflect the same kind of "country hopping" strategies that were planned over two decades ago when we retaliated for 9/11. 



Other than direct conflict, major world powers are only interested in defeating each other economically, and both China and Russia are currently hurting. Russia is bleeding wealth by funding their war in Ukraine, and China is suffering from their lack of a work force due to their previous one child policy and they are expected to collapse within the next 2 decades unless they make drastic changes. 



These are the cards on the table, and quite frankly this game is going to last for forever because nobody wants to lose, and nobody has the capability of guaranteed victory without substantially destroying most of the world.

### **@AdilMinocherhomjee** | 👍 102 likes

14:02 What’s wild is how none of this gets said out loud: the Ayatollah’s worldview is basically built on an ancient Persian religion called Zoroastrianism. It’s one of the oldest faiths in the world and it introduced the idea that the universe is a battle between good and evil, truth and lies, light and darkness. Literally the blueprint for end-of-days thinking.

This isn’t just Islamic theology…it’s cosmic storytelling from 3,000 years ago, still playing out in politics today.

And it gets even crazier: both Trump and Netanyahu have compared Trump to Cyrus the Great, a Persian king from the Bible who was also Zoroastrian.

Trump was cast as a modern Cyrus by evangelical Christian leaders who cited Isaiah 45 (Trump was the 45th U.S. President). They saw him as a flawed but divinely chosen instrument to “restore” America.

Netanyahu invoked Cyrus directly, saying Israel owes a moral debt to ancient Persia and linking that idea to Trump’s foreign policy toward Iran.

So across Iran, Israel, and the U.S., leaders are acting like they’re cast in some ancient myth. They’re borrowing symbols from a faith most people don’t even know exists and using them to justify real-world power plays.

This isn’t just politics. It’s spiritual theater with nuclear consequences.

Meanwhile, the actual Zoroastrians—myself included—are just living quietly, trying to be good humans, drinking chai, lighting a candle or two, and minding our own business. We’re not behind any of this. But thanks for keeping our metaphysics relevant, I guess.

> **@sharkman9500** | 👍 25 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bc9adtiIN_k&lc=Ugy_xu5uWbvT41gY0zN4AaABAg.AJoeZwx6rS1AJpIILhu_yR)  
>
> Actually, that’s a compelling narrative, but it oversimplifies both history and theology.  
> First the Ayatollah’s worldview is not based on Zoroastrianism, it's grounded in Shia theology, particularly Wilayat al-Faqih and that is Guardianship of the Islamic Jurist developed by Ayatollah Khomeini While Zoroastrianism may have influenced Persian culture but Islamic scholars including Shia clerics explicitly reject pre Islamic religions as sources of religious authority.The cosmic battle between good and evil exists in many religions; to say Zoroastrianism is the blueprint for Islamic eschatology ignores the Quran, Hadith, and centuries of Islamic scholarship.  
> Second, the Cyrus the Great comparisons don’t prove that Trump or Netanyahu are acting out Zoroastrian myths. Evangelicals in the U.S. used the Cyrus analogy to say that God can use even flawed leaders for good, a theme in Christian not Zoroastrian, theology. Netanyahu invoked Cyrus to highlight Jewish Persian history as recorded in the Bible not to align himself with Zoroastrian metaphysics. These are symbolic gestures, not signs of some secret cosmic roleplay.  
> Lastly, calling modern politics “spiritual theater with nuclear consequences” sounds poetic, but it undermines the very real strategic and geopolitical decisions being made. Leaders are not secretly channeling ancient myths they're using familiar stories to appeal to national identity, religion, or political base support.

> You’re right that Zoroastrianism had a huge influence on the ancient world, but the picture is more complex than a single source shaping everything.  
>   
> Zoroastrian ideas like final judgment and a cosmic struggle did influence Jewish thought after the Babylonian exile, but calling it the “original architecture” behind all modern morality oversimplifies a massive web of cultural exchange. Concepts like heaven, hell, justice, and resurrection also appeared independently in Egyptian, Mesopotamian, and early Greek traditions. These ideas weren’t exclusive to Zoroaster.  
>   
> Judaism, Christianity, and Islam evolved through conversations with many traditions, not just one. While Zoroastrian influence was real and especially during the Persian period but it’s a stretch to say modern political leaders are “enacting a structure Zoroastrianism built.” They’re referencing figures like Cyrus for symbolic or nationalistic reasons, not following an ancient religious script.  
>   
> The shared values between the Abrahamic faiths do matter. But the idea that Zoroastrianism is the root of all of it isn’t historical consensus.  
>   
> Yes, we share stories, but it’s not just one origin story it’s many things coming together over time.

> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bc9adtiIN_k&lc=Ugy_xu5uWbvT41gY0zN4AaABAg.AJoeZwx6rS1AJsfReY6BGX)  
>
> @sharkman9500  
>   
> Appreciate you engaging. Let’s go deeper and actually look at what Judaism believed before Persian contact, and how that changed once it came under Achaemenid rule.  
>   
> Early Judaism wasn’t monotheistic the way people assume today. It came out of Canaanite religion, which was polytheistic. Yahweh was one god among many. In Deuteronomy 32:8–9, especially in the earlier Septuagint and Dead Sea Scrolls versions, you see Elyon (the Most High) dividing up the nations among the “sons of God,” with Yahweh getting Israel as his portion. That’s not monotheism. That’s a divine council. You also see references to Asherah, a fertility goddess, being worshipped alongside Yahweh in Judah (see 2 Kings 21 and 2 Chronicles 33).¹  
>   
> At that stage, morality was tribal and covenantal. Do what Yahweh tells you, and you’re good. Break the covenant, and you suffer. But this wasn’t framed in terms of universal good and evil. And the afterlife? It was Sheol—a neutral, shadowy place where everyone went, righteous or wicked. No judgment. No heaven or hell. Just silence.  
>   
> Same thing shows up in Mesopotamian myth. In the Epic of Gilgamesh, death is final. The underworld is a dusty, miserable place. Everyone ends up there, no matter how they lived. There’s no moral sorting. The gods themselves are capricious.² In the Enuma Elish, Marduk creates order by destroying Tiamat, but it’s divine violence, not moral justice.  
>   
> Egyptian religion gets closer to moral judgment with the Weighing of the Heart in the Book of the Dead. Your heart is weighed against the feather of Ma’at (truth and order). If it’s heavier, you’re devoured. If it’s lighter, you move on. But in early periods, this was mostly reserved for pharaohs and elites. And the theology is still polytheistic. Time is cyclical. The Nile floods, the sun dies and rises. Everything returns.³  
>   
> Vedic India, which shares roots with Zoroastrianism, is also cyclical. The soul is reborn through samsara based on karma and dharma. There is morality, yes, but it plays out through reincarnation, not final judgment. Time is divided into yugas. You don’t get heaven or hell as moral destinations. You get rebirth. The goal is moksha, which is liberation from the cycle, not reward from a god.⁴  
>   
> Now here’s where Zoroastrianism is a clear break.  
>   
> Zarathustra shows up (most scholars place him somewhere between 1200 and 600 BCE) and introduces a radical shift:  
>   
> There is one god, Ahura Mazda, creator of all  
> There is a cosmic struggle between Asha (truth) and Druj (the lie)  
> Every human being has free will and moral responsibility  
> Souls are judged after death at the Chinvat Bridge  
> Good souls go to Best Existence, evil ones to Worst Existence  
> There is a final renovation of the world called Frashokereti  
> A savior, Saoshyant, will come to lead this renewal⁵  
>   
> That’s a moral system with cosmic consequences. It ties ethics to metaphysics. No tribalism. No elite-only access. This system is universal, linear, and teleological. It says the world is going somewhere, and what you do matters forever.  
>   
> Now look what happens after 539 BCE, when Cyrus the Great conquers Babylon. He frees the Jews from exile and lets them return to Judah. From that point until 330 BCE, the Jewish people live under Persian rule.  
>   
> And during that exact period, Jewish theology changes in dramatic ways:  
>   
> Satan becomes a prosecuting adversary (see the Book of Job)  
> Heaven and hell start appearing as destinations based on moral judgment  
> Resurrection shows up in Daniel 12  
> Apocalyptic literature begins spreading (Book of Enoch, Qumran texts)  
> Messianic ideas become central to Jewish thought⁶  
>   
> These concepts are all Zoroastrian in structure and timing. Before exile, they’re absent. After exposure to Persian rule, they’re everywhere.  
>   
> This isn’t random development. It’s cultural and theological contact. And scholars across traditions—Mary Boyce, Albert de Jong, Shaul Shaked—have all shown these overlaps are too specific and too well-timed to be coincidence.⁷ Even Richard Carrier, in peer-reviewed work, argues that post-exilic Judaism incorporated Persian dualism and eschatology in direct response to imperial influence.⁸  
>   
> To be clear, this doesn’t mean Judaism copied Zoroastrianism wholesale. It means Judaism evolved through contact, absorbing and adapting ideas from a dominant empire’s worldview. Then Christianity and Islam inherited and expanded that framework.  
>   
> So yeah—ideas like moral judgment, resurrection, and the final battle between good and evil didn’t just come from “elsewhere.” They were refined, systematized, and exported through Zoroastrianism. That’s what made them stick.  
>   
> Judaism didn’t invent those concepts.  
> It canonized them after Persia gave them form.  
>   
> ⸻

> ² Andrew George, The Epic of Gilgamesh (Penguin Classics)  
> ³ Geraldine Pinch, Egyptian Myth: A Very Short Introduction  
> ⁴ Gavin Flood, An Introduction to Hinduism  
> ⁵ Mary Boyce, Zoroastrians: Their Religious Beliefs and Practices  
> ⁶ John J. Collins, Apocalypticism in the Dead Sea Scrolls  
> ⁷ Albert de Jong, Traditions of the Magi: Zoroastrianism in Greek and Latin Literature  
> ⁸ Richard C. Carrier, “Zoroaster and the Jews,” Kritikos, peer-reviewed journal, 2005

It's not the Messianic calling that makes them so energetic. People who haven't worked with mentally ill people really struggle to understand how the mentally ill feel. I see how one can confuse a Messiah complex with Messianic calling. The reason they seem to have so much energy is that their minds won't leave them alone. That's how someone can be awake for 2 weeks without sleeping or using any drugs. That's how someone can wear out everyone around them. Idk about the other leaders, but DT has very clearcut signs of narcissistic personality disorder. With NPD he feels like he doesn't exist. Think of it as having negative self-confidence. And there's an infinite desperation to keep his head above water despite constantly sinking.

DT's constant campaigning is because he has found a strong source for narcissistic supply. Boastful speech is really a soothing behavior for a person with NPD. It's self-administering of narcissistic supply. Even better if a crowd is standing there cheering for you. While praise and admiration will make a normal person feel flattered, a person with NPD feels elated to reach a neutral state of confidence. The more pressed and under fire the narcissist feel, the more boastful he becomes and the more he seeks admiration from those around him. The more desperate he becomes, the more demanding, aggressive and abusive he becomes. So this is also where the abuse and aggression comes from when he feels threatened.

However, if the narcissist gets +10 in narcissistic supply, it drops by -15 soon after, which is why narcissists become restless. They're admiration junkies, and each shot feels less and less, and coming off the high gets worse and worse. This is also why they need to constantly elevate the admiration. Each shot needs to be more than the last in order to achieve neutral. And this is where the Messiah complex comes in. Many narcissists who achieve cult leader or similar status often start to call themselves god or Jesus or something like that. First they're "a teacher of truth", then they become "the only one god speaks through", and then they eventually declare themselves god. This doesn't meant they truly feel or believe that, it's just that's how much narcissistic supply they need to feel normal. They need to feel like they have the power of god just to not feel like shit.

And this is where the restlessness comes from, because if they stop swimming, they drown. And that's their only concern. It's an infinite desperation to exist. So he doesn't really think he's working for the people or god or anything like that. He's primed to constantly boost himself up as he desperately tries to survive.

### **@ayan-yuegupta3983** | 👍 9 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bc9adtiIN_k&lc=UgywmoO6D-M5TBoctyB4AaABAg)

I'm not sure how useful this kind of speculation on internal mental states is. There's just not really enough evidence to say anything as specific as 'these people have a messianic complex'. The question of how individual motivations is interesting, but fundamentally unknowable -- all we can do is make inferences about macro-level *political* or *economic* interests (of states, corporations etc.) from history, knowledge of countries' domestic affairs, knowledge of economics etc., and we know that individual *personal* motivations will be roughly aligned with these. 

Trump's aggression towards Iran reflects decades of history of US empire installing client regimes in the middle east, both to prevent oil prices being used as a weapon against the USA (like the 1973 oil shock), and to prevent dedollarisation and prevent China undermining its unipolar hegemony (Iran has already been selling oil in Yuan and is a member of BRICS). 

Why would Trump do a kind of accelerationist bluff designed to end US interference by doing more interference in Iran? Why wouldn't he just stop interfering (e.g. stop all aid to Israel)? Surely a simpler explanation is that Trump is simply an extension of the US interventionist foreign policy that has been around since post-WW2?

Netanyahu is riding the wave of genocidal Israeli settler-colonialism by expanding war (attacks on Syria, Yemen, Lebanon, Iran), both because he believes in it and because it keeps him in power. The messianic calling idea is most plausible here to be fair, just because of how insane Zionism is. 

Khamenei's actions again reflects a century of Iran trying to maintain some semblance of sovereignty. Throughout the 20th century Iran has been interfered with by western imperialism, and Iran's actions since the 1979 revolution have been a response to this. One only has to look the history of at colonial ownership of it's oil, US backed coup of Mossadegh, US backed rule of the Shah, sanctions...

I can see how the messianic calling idea comes from the Christian Zionism stuff, there is definitely a messianic streak in US politics. But I don't think you can generalise that from Christian Zionism and make messianic calling a central explanation of what's happening. Ultimately what's going on is the USA trying to maintain its unipolar status, with Israel as a proxy to achieve this. This is a bipartisan interest, embedded in both Republicans and Democrats, that has been around since the cold war. So it's 'beyond' any particular individual.

>
> Great take, thanks for sharing. I'm also skeptical of the extent to which we can predict macro-level patterns from micro-level psychological states. I do think it's fair to say that most powerful leaders have narcissistic and/or sociopathic tendencies, but this as you pointed out can simply mean that their personal, narcissistic motivations are aligned with the economic interests of states and corporations (which are themselves sociopathic entities). At which point the economic interests of states/corporations are still central to predicting geopolitical developments, not internal states.   
>   
> At the same time, I'm not a strict historical materialist and I do believe psychology plays a role in world events. But I think we need to look more to group psychology or even notions of collective consciousness that emerge through the interactions of individual minds with culture, tech, and the economy. This can get very fuzzy/woo if we're not careful, as the science of studying collective consciousness, emergent networks of intelligence, etc. is still in its infancy. But I do believe that to be a missing link for how we resolve the age old idealism/materialism contradiction.   
>   
> To end this post on a hopeful note, if we can see our own actions and words as contributing to a collective consciousness/intelligence that can help bring humanity beyond the short-sighted zero-sum competition that has caused such disaster (admittedly also technological development) for the last thousands of years, this can provide a sense of meaning that is not dependent on belief in an afterlife or supernatural forces. This is still ultimately a hypothetical, but I believe the concepts of emergence and network theory, as well as simply observing evolution's "progress" towards more complex forms of life and intelligence, provide a rational basis for belief in an intelligence that includes but transcends our individual minds, and brings us out of the nihilism of separation. See Michael Levin's work for more on this.  

> **@ayan-yuegupta3983**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bc9adtiIN_k&lc=UgywmoO6D-M5TBoctyB4AaABAg.AJnUiMJyofbAJpXrnVv5aI)  
>
> ​@brianh5844 Thanks for your response! Agreed that the question of how individual interests 'aggregate' into collective interests is important. I tend to think of this issue from a pragmatist philosophy of language perspective, in which group interests come from the negotiation of individual interests through Wittgensteinian language games. So to understand emergence of group motivations from individual motivations, one first has to understand communication in terms of speech-acts (e.g. assertions, questions, promise-making etc.), which are inherently intersubjective in that they externalise individual motivations, beliefs, etc., and so open them up to public evaluation and response. If I make an assertion 'X is red', I open up a personal belief  for public evaluation, and the belief gets transformed into a proposition for which I can be held accountable, i.e. I am responsible for justifying it.   
>   
> Then one can model macroscopic social phenomena as large scale language games in which the atomic moves are speech acts (just as the atomic moves in a chess game involves things like 'you are permitted to move your knight 3 squares vertically and 1 square horizontally'). And then individual motives get translated into group motivations by being processed by long sequences of people making and responding to speech acts. I'm essentially following Robert Brandom's views on language and sociality here.   
>   
> Thanks for the lead on Michael Levin, looks very interesting. He's the synthetic biology guy right? Always found synthetic biology fascinating. Regarding the relation between a sense of meaning and the crises we are going through, I do hold a much more materialist position -- I don't think these crises are a result of a lack of meaning that encompasses humanity in general, I think they are more the result of a small, powerful, capitalist/neoimperialist class. So a widespread turn towards a more rational sense of collective being wouldn't change much in my opinion, if it isn't accompanied by political struggle that results in the defeat of this class. Is this even possible? I don't know, I'm pretty pessimistic about this!  

> **@brianh5844**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bc9adtiIN_k&lc=UgywmoO6D-M5TBoctyB4AaABAg.AJnUiMJyofbAJqD9Bf5aNc)  
>
> ​@ayan-yuegupta3983 I wonder if you can see a link between the might makes right, domination/power at all costs world view that this capitalist/neoimperialist class holds and a lack of meaning? In my understanding, meaning is an innate human psychological need. It is not some fluffy, ethereal concept with no grounding in material reality. It is based in real relationships and a real understanding of one's interconnectedness with the larger system and web of life one finds oneself in. And lastly, it is based in love of some kind, another concept that, depending on the extent of your materialist leanings, may also seem abstract or woo, but I believe to be as real as photons or evolution.   
>   
> The narcissistic and sociopathic characters that largely run the world are lacking in two essential human needs: meaning and love, and as such, they instead become fixated on and addicted to the thrills of power and domination. Would you want to be Elon Musk? Most (sane) people would not, seeing that he is miserable, insecure, and whose life completely lacking in genuine, meaningful relationships, instead everything is transactional. His addiction to power and attention is no different than a smack addict, though with far more harmful consequences for the rest of humanity. I believe that this is self-evident to anyone who takes the time to consider what motivates people in positions of power, how they tick, and what makes them different than someone like yourself that has a wish for more prosperity for all. Unfortunately, because of the understandable anger this class provokes, this analysis often doesn't go further than "they're bad" or "it doesn't really matter, they need to be taken out."   
>   
> I can understand at this point how it might seem like I'm making an argument similar to that of the professor's that you responded to, one that centralizes individuals and their psychology to explain macro-level political realities. However, allow me to elucidate what I'm saying further: I hope you'll open yourself to a different way of looking at this tension between micro and macro level explanations for phenomena.   
>   
> The reason that what I'm saying about this class and their behavioral motivations matters for a vision of social change is that we should be understanding these behaviors and psyches as outgrowths of the larger systemic realities, which themselves are tied to a long history in our biology of domination, predation, and parasitism *as well as* mutualism, symbiosis, cooperation, and even love. If we recognize that all of these potentials exist within each one of us, and design systems that attempt to maximize our prosocial tendencies and meet our deepest human needs for connection, belonging, meaning, and love, then we have a better chance of not reproducing horrors even after one particular group of people expressing antisocial traits are defeated.   
>   
> If we think the root of the problem is in a specific class of people rather than the systemic conditions that led to the emergence of those power relationships, and the ways that people were led astray from a more fulfilling, connected reality, then we're actually committing the same fallacy as the professor, locating the problem in the internality of individuals. The real issue is not those specific people, but the problem of the potentials for sociopathy, predation, addiction, and domination in the human being.  
>   
> Of course, the basic problem remains that these potentials have been realized in this group of people who are currently dominating the majority of us. And yes, if by "defeat" we mean ending the systems of domination that enable their rule, I would agree that defeat is necessary. But how we arrive at that defeat needn't rely on the same might-makes-right worldview that characterizes the domination system. Certainly, we need collective power and solidarity. But the extent to which we need focus our attention on these individuals versus practicing prefigurative politics and simply starting the work of building different systems, modes of production, and more harmoniously relating to the natural world is, I believe, up for debate.   
>   
> I wrote all of that and yet I realize I didn't respond to your explanation of Wittgensteinian language games and how they provide an explanation for the bridge from individual to collective motivations. It's interesting stuff, but my initial (novice) response as someone trying out the idea is that reducing everything to language seems quite limiting. Certainly, language must play a huge role, and it may well be a useful model for understanding how language plays out in group dynamics, but I know from experience that group dynamics are also greatly influenced by more subtle factors like body language and what is not being said as much as what is being said.   
>   
> I've also had the experience (in the midst of a particularly horrific bout of collective violence in 2020) of feeling my individual consciousness subsumed into something like a collective consciousness. It's something that is difficult to explain except to say that it felt a bit like being on drugs that temporarily soften or remove one's ego (psilocybin and the like), but more terrifying, and I assure you I was quite sober. That was a very formative experience for me, because something happened to my consciousness that was unlike anything I had experienced before, and what I experienced was very close to a feeling of total loss of control despite the fact that no one was speaking at the time. I mention it only to say that while language games may be a good starting point, I suspect there is quite a lot more going on that is non-verbal. That may be where Michael Levin comes in, in particular his work on collective intelligence and the ways in which we can find intelligence in all sorts of non-verbal biological phenomena and at all sorts of different scales. As humans that are definitely animals, we have our own group mind/hive mind/swarm dynamics, whatever you want to call it, and I think we are a long ways from fully understanding how these function. In any case, check out this paper for a good introduction to some of his work: https://www.nature.com/articles/s42003-024-06037-4  
>   
> This is now going beyond Levin, and I know this has unfortunate associations with some new age ideas, but I would not be surprised if the resonance and harmonization of EM fields that we emit (both from our brains and hearts) was involved somehow in group dynamics, for example. In other words, "vibes" might actually be a real thing. Some animals do use EM fields in ordering group behavior so it is at least a testable hypothesis. I suspect you may not take me too seriously, but I hope you'll at least entertain my ideas, as I have entertained yours. I recognize that we have a shared interest in collective liberation, and I appreciate your continued perseverance and effort in the face of what seem like such distressing odds.

### **@BigMoneyMayo_** | 👍 12 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bc9adtiIN_k&lc=Ugw8vO76SjDcdJ6kLzF4AaABAg)

This guy knows just enough to sound credible while pushing a carefully constructed narrative that doesn’t hold up under scrutiny. Let’s be clear: This video isn’t real analysis, it’s a dramatic, speculative story wrapped in historical references and rhetorical flair.

Yes, he’s clearly informed. He drops names, dates, and historical context that sound convincing. But he also selectively frames the facts, ignores counterpoints, and projects a symbolic lens onto global politics that distorts more than it reveals.

Framing Trump, Khamenei, and Netanyahu as literal messianic figures on divine missions isn’t grounded in evidence, at all. it’s more like creative theory or metaphorical fan fiction. While the religious undertones of their support bases are real, reducing their geopolitical decisions to cosmic destiny overlooks the real-world factors that matter most: strategy, economics, domestic politics, diplomacy, and military constraints.

He connects ancient history and fringe beliefs to modern events in a way that’s intriguing but ultimately misleading. It makes war sound like fate, not policy. And that’s a dangerous oversimplification. We do not want more war. 

This is what I’d call the illusion of insight: it feels profound, but it’s more narrative than reality. Don’t confuse confidence with clarity. It’s not prophecy. It’s just one theory, and it’s far from the full picture.

Fascinating. You really pull in the individual's perspectives in how the nations are polarized towards events by those individual's actions. 

As a person who is admittedly aligned with this 'messianic calling' I can agree with most of that. However, I am not delusional so I create a 'framework' of mind that neutralizes illogical and unreasonable actions and perspectives in 'life'. This alters the mentality to 'require' impossible signatures that would fuel that condition, the 'messianic calling'. Fortunately this removes any connection to 'religious' concepts, but also makes me question systems like science and financial systems as being 'religious' because they are often related to 'unreasonable' actions and outcomes. The 'science' is a good 'foundation' of reasonable perspective, but my analytical mind and 'messianic calling' perspective seems to force me to find 'flaws' within science, which I often do and then experiment to find the solutions to those 'flaws', but only through verifiable and validated tangible and applicable outcomes. I 'use' this 'divine energy' by concept to conduct research and discovery, as well as skill development and this becomes a 'tight rope' to walk on. It makes me 'eccentric' at times, but still very rooted in 'how reality is' as we all experience it. 

However, many of those 'impossible thresholds' have been 'crossed' by verifiable evidence, which has been very difficult to explain because they are categorized as 'miracles' and can be reproduced to a degree, in some cases every effort to do so, as a skillful expression. I attempted to counter this, so that it doesn't 'alter' my conditional perspective on the world, by recognize the event 'as it is' but also allowing myself to see myself as not 'having to alter myself' to be a condition of that undetermined 'messianic calling'. 

Another thing I found interesting about this 'mindset' is how it attempt to twist prophetic conditions towards a personal circumstance. That is as to align the self personification to 'embody' the described prophetic individual who does 'whatever' according to that prophecy. It's fairly fascinating, and it's easy for me to 'dismiss' the situation by accepting the idea that 'If I were this person described here, I wouldn't be required to do anything outside of who I already am because they would be speaking of me, not outlining who I 'should be''. That meaning that if I 'react' to the prophetic story in such a way to 'become' that individual, then I am clearly not that individual because I would only be 'acting' and not 'being' in such a way. That seems to be the most logical neutralization of such a perspective so it does not gain momentum in a mind to become 'self inflicted' by this condition of 'messianic calling'. 

Why I have this condition is very ingrained with me because of circumstances directly experienced before I even went to school and learned the alphabet, how to read, science, and all those other 'normal' understandings in this world. Furthermore, experiences that are 'unexplained' but also witnessed become 'feeders' to this condition that require me to find 'scientific' or 'reasonable explanations' to understand. Unfortunately, this has focused my research and studies in areas outside of 'normal science' where I gain a lot of understanding in the same way that I explained previously, having both tangible and applicable outcomes, though these can be explained, replicated, and enjoyed. This specifically pushed my research in what people call 'glitches' and these are very logical conditions that can do things that are not accepted by standard science. So then my occupation to outline how these conditions and applied processes work takes up my time, and possibly will help in some way given to that messianic calling condition to 'be the one who does this or that' for some purpose, which would be to outline new sciences the world can use, and given to the strict skeptical conditioning I place on myself because these are so far removed from standard sciences, it would be true. 

It seems that it is possible that given to our more ancient individuals who might have been 'hermetic' in their lives, that they became 'monks', focusing on research that did end up being a part of our scientific understanding. Perhaps in the future, when we find this in individuals, it can be cultivated to a more functional placement of their efforts in such a way. The line between sanity and insanity is recognizing that we can become self inflicted, we are vulnerable to our own internal and external personified allowances. To know that I am flawed, and to be vigilant in my footing, while not attempting to completely deny who I am, or of my natural state of existing, being as I am so to speak. 

I figured since you mentioned this and I recognize this in myself, I can attempt to explain this condition first hand. The mental acrobatics involved to not become delusional is rigid and strict, often cycling but also maintaining the center of sanity with proper parameters in place. Perhaps everyone does this to a degree, so that we don't just fall down those rabbit holes just waiting for the curious seeking answers to impossible questions.

> Before understanding this principle I often made theories on specific aspects of science, only able to use 'logic and reason' and what I believed to be true by previous researchers. I could not specifically attend the labs, so I had to work with what I have, my conscious awareness of the principles given to the measure of that specific concept I was considering. In this, I made a few statements between the laws of science made, it was the 'only answer' given to the science at the moment. I wrote this down and a year later the 'discovery' of that exact theory was made, announced, and praised.  
>   
> I went back to recognize that I did indeed write exactly what was discovered, and also showed a few family members so they could give me an more objective view of what I was considering. However, after going through it, I realized something didn't fit, with what I had learned in that year. I altered the fundamental base of that theory, I was wrong about what I 'thought' I discovered, but then I had to ask myself 'why' or 'how' did others make the same mistake. Obviously I could make mistakes because I didn't have the 'equipment' nor the access to other scientists of alike mind, but they did. So that I did not give myself praise for being just as good as their entire research lab, cost analysis considerations to my own value, I chose to ignore what I could not directly observe, but also outline the current understanding of my scientific consideration to what was discovered. It changed the principle of that discovery. A few weeks later, so did they also change their discovery, which had they used equipment, the later change I made would have been obvious because they had the equipment to measure by observation which I did not. That was just what led me to take further consideration in how this was possible, because it wasn't the 'first time' I had observed this kind of thing.   
>   
> Not to get into too much more, but since it is the baseline of your misunderstanding, or my lack of explanation, I should elaborate so there is no more confusion on the matter.   
>   
> I began to search in other theories where observations and or discoveries would shift or alternate in such a way. Of course I came across several studies that spoke about 'observing outcomes' and 'changes' to the outcomes by the observer. I needed to understand this through 'strong tangible and applicable' experimentation to 'know' what I know is or isn't, what I believe it to be. I 'have to' understand this, how it works, why it is this way, and to what end. It's not an easy thing to research without tools, but then the 'observer' in these theories is the tool in some ways.   
>   
> That was over a decade ago, so many experiments were made where I could find the fissures between laws, then 'make up' a reasonable and logical conclusion that would bind these laws together, in a sensible way. It's not that they were needed to explain the principles of 'how these laws worked' but rather to see if there could be 'more than one' explanation that would contradict the others. I first had to establish the link to others' discovery, which seemed to be expected. When I made a discovery, wrote it down, soon there after, it would be published where 'soon' is a relative term in the measure of 'time'. Regardless, when this did happen, I could contrast that 'discovery' with the other contrasting idea, and dismiss the previous, and following the discovery would be 'updated', and this is fascinating, to a degree.   
>   
> What was then needed, after several times doing that previous, is if contrasting discoveries could 'alter' the outcome by principle. That meaning, it was still a logical and reasonable expression from the more 'base' law, to the consequential layer. That would be like to say this one law could only exist if the previous law is true, but between the laws there could be a 'discovery' of many different outcomes based solely on the foundation of these two laws working together. So when I came up with 'logical and reasonable' outcomes on the 'base law' that would 'alter' the following to some degree, a new measure in contrasting results could be made, and this became true, however not without 'consequence'. The preset in what was 'constructed' or created by the previous sequence would 'shift' by consequence by the distance between 'cause and effect' through 'time'. This meaning the 'new discovery' between those laws would then become the 'new determinate outcome' thus polarizing 'effect' to that cause. We would either see a general trend of consequence or a complete shift people would call 'glitches' which became predictable.   
>   
> This is not unlike early ancient beliefs in those tribes that believed the 'sun' might be angry at them for 'action A' to cause 'effect B' like a drought. Their 'belief' was the effect they polarized. I believe that few people learned of this concept in the same way I did. Where their omnipotent concept would have 'known' the 'truth' in reality, so their mistake was then obvious, but the 'drought' still occurred. Their own 'mistake' was expressed, not the omnipotent consequence. They realized that it was the 'belief' that held the power of consequence, not the 'personified object' like the 'sun' itself. This can become a 'powerful tool' but very difficult to measure, though not impossible.   
>   
> So that's why I mean when I say that science is more like a 'religion' not to belittle it, but to recognize it. It is 'humanity' that I belittle, not the nature of existence.

### **@JuniorPlan**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bc9adtiIN_k&lc=UgyROu4R5ppzH1Rb78F4AaABAg)

You mentioned three individuals who are viewed by many as having received a messianic calling, fulfilling a symbolic role as deliverers or restorers. In this sense, the term messianic refers not to the divine Messiah in the theological sense, but to someone elevated as a savior figure in political or cultural narratives.

As a Catholic, I recognize the Pope as the Vicar of Christ, and we are familiar with figures like Moses, who played a divinely appointed role in salvation history. However, we are also warned of false prophets, those who proclaim themselves or are proclaimed by others as God’s chosen or anointed, leading people astray under the guise of deliverance.

Historical figures like Napoleon Bonaparte and Adolf Hitler embraced or were adorned with messianic imagery, often portraying themselves as instruments of divine or historical destiny. They used language, symbolism, and myth to craft a narrative that they alone could restore order or greatness. In doing so, they weaponized hope, collapsed critical thought, and demanded loyalty to their person over principle.

These false messianic roles did not emerge in a vacuum, they were enabled by societal decay, crises, and a longing for redemption. That pattern echoes in our time. Political figures today, across ideologies and nations, are again being cast in this savior mold. They are wrapped in religious or civilizational language, and they attract followers willing to overlook contradictions for the sake of a perceived mission.

Which brings me to the question. Is it important to study past figures like Napoleon and Hitler, who embodied a messianic role, not only to understand the strategic games at play in their time, but also to refine our use of game theory in analyzing similar patterns today?

### **@HunterAkihara** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bc9adtiIN_k&lc=UgxKs7aTjTlNjmK5FV14AaABAg)

This is a compelling narrative, but I think there's a deeper issue worth exploring. When a theory can apply equally to Trump, Netanyahu, and Khamenei, figures from radically different religious, cultural, and geopolitical contexts, without needing meaningful adjustment, it risks becoming too flexible to be explanatory. In philosophy of science, this is sometimes called being “easy to vary”: if a model can stretch to fit anything, it stops helping us understand something specific.

That doesn’t mean the thesis is wrong, just that it might be operating more like a psychological metaphor than a constrained explanatory model. I’d be curious to see this developed further with tighter distinctions: what would falsify the “messianic” claim in each case? What unique behaviors emerge from this belief that couldn’t be explained through strategic rationality, institutional pressure, or historical contingency?

In short, it’s a rich framework, but to really work, it needs constraint, not just resonance.

> **@oozly9114** | 👍 1 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bc9adtiIN_k&lc=UgxKs7aTjTlNjmK5FV14AaABAg.AJoXJpGowXEAJoXgEH8Ctu)  
>
> I think it applies because they are all praying to the god of Abraham.  

> **@HunterAkihara**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bc9adtiIN_k&lc=UgxKs7aTjTlNjmK5FV14AaABAg.AJoXJpGowXEAJoYF2cwU-F)  
>
> ​@oozly9114Agreed. But also, one layer I think is often missing from theories like this, and this applies well beyond this video, is the role of contingency and logistics. Even if leaders hold messianic beliefs, their actions are always filtered through bureaucracies, material bottlenecks, tactical limitations, and situational constraints. A messianic leader might want to ignite a clash of civilizations, but if troop deployments are delayed, funding is blocked, or regional allies push back, that desire gets rerouted or deferred. The causal chain from belief to outcome isn’t linear; it’s mediated by infrastructure, timing, and friction. In that sense, the failure to model these logistical and contingent dimensions makes the thesis vulnerable, not because it’s wrong ideologically, but because it skips the grind of how history actually moves. I’d love to see future analyses layer in that structural depth,not just refining the belief models, but also testing them against the real-world frictions that determine whether intentions actually materialize.

I joined ytube in order to subscribe and comment. Very interesting content and delivery. Today we have too much bombastic content with dangerous hyperbole and shallow analysis.

In saying that, whilst I respect the assertions being made, I think there is further room for more balance, and some of the assertions being made (such as Trump and Net's motivations) are problematic. You mentioned early on that they were not motivated by power, but this was left unexamined. 

We might also say that Trump's followers believe Trump to be motivated by God, or by dismantling the so-called Deep State, but it doesnt necessarily follow that this is what Trump believes.

I would also challenge some of the historical claims made in this talk, namely the references to Israel, some of which relies on religious texts which are historically flawed. 

Further context about the historical land of Palestine, including that which predates the supposedly Biblical claim to the land, would be welcomed.

Great style and much of the content is balanced, which is more than we can say for 99% of all media these days.

The Angel Who Let the Apple Fall from the Tree 

It came to pass that an apple fell from the tree. Isaac did not turn his eyes toward the earth, but toward the abyss that yawned within his heart. The fruit taught him nothing; it only stirred the knowledge he had carried before time.

It was not intuition—it was the memory of the first breath. Isaac was born Adam, not the first, but the heir of the first. And the Book awakened within him.

Raziel had already inscribed it upon his breath. And history repeated itself, under another name. He entered the world with the Book open, not to read it, but to have drunk it before he even drew breath.

He discovered nothing—he remembered, like a child who hums a lullaby never taught.

The key dwelled in his spirit, and he saw the world in its hidden truth. Yet men, calling themselves wise, shattered that spirit. Thus the perdition foretold by Enoch came to pass: the apple became law, the law became machine, and the machine became bomb. Enoch had written it. The angels transmitted the knowledge. Men forged the sword.

Adam received the Book. They believed he had come to sow gardens. But he was there to forge the end. It was not punishment, but revelation.

MartOn Nov 08, 2025

### **@MiravusVideos**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bc9adtiIN_k&lc=UgxH6XnJi6e-p7ZDV-B4AaABAg)

How do you contend with the whole mess of historical precedent that heavily disputes the idea that Zionist leaders have anything close to a Messianic vision of themselves and their broader mission?

From the start, Zionism was never a religious movement. Zionism gets its start in the mid-1800s among wealthy Jews in Western Europe looking to emulate the colonial successes of the great powers for themselves. (Remember, at the time, colonization is viewed as this amazing force of progress because they largely view the indigenous people being colonized as backwards or deserving of repression. Look no further than the British colonial mission in India.)  The early Zionists both speak explicitly in these terms and also explicitly eschew religious backing for their movement.  All of the early heads of Zionism were intensely secular, and actually Zionism faced tremendous pushback from religious Jews for about 100 years before the Holocaust brought them into the fold (for which I don't think they can be blamed, as the targets of genocide, after all, you take any ticket out, even if it's a ticket from people who you for 100 years considered to be in direct violation of religious dictate).  Even the decision to pick Palestine was a purely political one, driven by the ease of propagandizing a biblical "homeland" as a target for settlement as opposed to places in Africa they had been considering like Uganda.  Even that, though, was not the whole story, because the resistance faced from the indigenous population was considered to be harsher in Africa than Palestine, favoring settlement in Palestine.  That said, early Zionists are also very clear in their views that the only way for their settler project to be successful is the eradication of the indigenous population in region. Read Vladimir Zabotinski to see how all of the core aspects of the Israeli push for control of the region have been baked into Zionism completely apart from any religious compunction.


This video strikes me as one made in stark ignorance of the longer history of Zionism and the relations it bears to virtually every other settler-colonial project, all of which also used similar propaganda and saw similarly eschatological pushes to consume their societies in all-out combat for their survival.  The two best examples of this historically are the US with manifest destiny (I don't think you'll argue any President viewed themselves as Messianic, or at least that such a view was entirely separate from the material reality of pushing for either total genocide at the potential cost of the total destruction of the settler state), and Nazi Germany with their Lebensraum (which again I don't expect that anyone views as being the result of Messianic sympathies).

There are far more robust, far more evidenceable, far more testable and verifiable explanations for why Israel and Iran act the way they do than this idealistic view of the conflict as resultant from a supposedly Messianic calling simultaneously shared by virtually every relevant leader in a conflict that, by this point, now spans well over 50 years.  The evidence needed to show that this is what was going on would truly be staggering, and it's just not provided in this video.

I also think it's very silly to think that Trump has any sort of Messianic calling, he is just blatantly self-interested, and now suffering a bit from the typical mental degeneration you get when you're 80+ years old.  Unless the argument is that Trump somehow acquired this calling in the last decade or so, but that also doesn't track with Messianic calling, does it?  Trump cares absolutely nothing about religion or god, come on.  This is the guy that cannot even name a single verse in the bible.  Why on earth do you think that a person who views the bible more as a good opportunity for a photo op than anything else would believe they're the chosen of god.  This seems like an interesting fiction, but functionally unverifiable, and deeply disconnected both with the history of fascist movements (which generate these exact kinds of conflicts regularly) and specifically the history of the conflicts involving Israel and Iran in the middle east.

Like, how are you doing a video about a conflict between the US, Israel, and Iran without talking about the overthrow of Mosaddegh, the US and Israel supporting radical Islamism in Iran as a lever to overthrow a democratically-elected government, the LONG history of fascism and seeking armageddon, or the history of Zionist expansion in Palestine and the role it has played in destabilizing indigenous power structures in the region?

> When Iran uses the phrase "Death to America" (Persian: "Marg bar Âmrikâ"), it is generally not intended as a literal wish for the death of American people. Instead, Iranian leaders and many Iranians explain it as a condemnation of U.S. government policies and actions that they view as hostile, arrogant, and interfering in Iran's affairs.  Ali Khamenei, have explicitly stated that "Death to America" means "death to the U.S.'s policies, death to arrogance," and "death to the few American rulers who are running that country." They cite historical grievances such as the 1953 U.S.-backed coup that overthrew Iran's democratically elected prime minister, Mohammad Mossadegh, and the subsequent support for the autocratic Shah. They also point to sanctions, U.S. military presence in the region, and support for adversaries.
